Premier: Government To Fulfill Obligations to
Pensioners

LD1410145795 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1322 GMT 14 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, Oct 14 (INTER-
FAX) — The Russian government will fulfill all of its
obligations regarding the country’s pensioners, Prime
Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin promised at a meeting
with representatives of the Union of Russia’s Pension-
ers today.

"If there are obligations, they have to be fulfilled,”
Chernomyrdin said. He said funds for implementing
a number of decisions concerning the indexing of
pensions had not been fixed in the 1995 federal budget.
"To carry out all what was promised, we will have to
find additional 7-8 trillion rubles by the end of this year
and we will certainly find this money," Chernomyrdin
said.

Chernomyrdin said the government would also bring
in line with inflation wages of the public sector's
employees. He said the government had taken special
control of the situation concerning delayed payment of
pensions “which considerably improved the situation
during the last month and a half." "Much depends on the
Russian regions, as far as this problem is concerned,"
he said.

At the meeting, the Union of Russia’s Pensioners
proposed that “homes of pensioners” be built in Moscow
and other Russian cities and that financial support
be provided to the Union. Chernomyrdin was also
introduced a program for creating in Russia a unified
system for paying pensions drafted as an alternative
to the program proposed by the Ministry for Social
Security of the Population. The Union of Russia’s
Pensioners said the implementation of its program
would help raise the size of average pensions by two
times due to a more rational use of existing funds
without attracting additional money for resolving this
problem. ;

Chernomyrdin said the government would examine the
proposals in detail. He said “we will use all which is
useful, all which is needed."

Chernomyrdin, who heads the Our Home is Russia,
hailed the Union of Russia’s Pensioners joining this
electoral bloc.

Chernomyrdin: 1995 Overall Industrial Output To
Fall 3%

LD1410144795 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1322 GMT 14 Oct 95

{FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 14 (INTER-
FAX) — Decline in Russian °95 annual overall indus-
trial output will be 3% instead of the expected 6-8%,
Russian Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin said at a
meeting with representatives of the Union of Russia’s
Pensioners in Moscow today.

Chernomyrdin said he believed that in 1996 Russian
industrial production would rise.

Chernomyrdin said Russia had managed to bring
monthly inflation down from 18% in January to 4.5%
in September. "We will come close to the planned
figures at 1-2% by the end of this year," he said.

With regards to measures for supporting duped in-
vestors, Chernomyrdin said a special fund was being
created for compensating investors a part of their finan-
cial losses caused by such companies as the MMM infa-
mous pyramid-type investment fund, the Russkiy Dom
Selenga and the Khoper-Invest. He said the government
would also financially support investment companies
which "respect all state regulatory requirements” when
working with the population’s money.

With regards to the flight of capital, Chernomyrdin said
from $6 billion to $8 billion were annually removed
beyond Russia’s borders. He said these were mostly
funds of exporters which left part of money abroad to
pay for future contracts.

Chernomyrdin said he believed that the Russian popu-
lation currently had $15-20 billion in cash.

Chernomyrdin also praised the discussion of the °96
draft federal budget in the State Duma Friday. "If we
had worked like that earlier, we would have progressed
much better. This was a normal, constructive and
business-like conversation,” he said.

Chernomyrdin confirmed the government’s intention
to look for compromises with the State Duma on
the °96 draft federal budget within the scope of the
reconciliatory commission.

in Sochi President Yeltsin offered him the post of prime

the team that will manage to attain the goals set to it,"
Skokov declared.

confidence that the president had really wanted to do
something good for his people, who had elected him.
"Boris Yeltsin is our contemporary history. And I am
against spitting on our present history alike," he said.

Baturin Sees Crime, Islamic Factor as Key Security
Threats

MMI1310154695 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian
14 Oct 95 pl

[Report by Vasiliy Kononenko on press conference
given by Presidential National Security Adviser Yuriy
Baturin at RIA NOVOSTI in Moscow on 13 October:
“League of Criminals and Civil Servants Threatens
National Security")

[FBIS Translated Text] Perhaps for the first time since
Russia acquired state sovereignty, it has been stated at
official level that we have no national security concept
and none can be expected in the near future. This
statement was made by Presidential National Security
Adviser Yu. Baturin at a meeting with journalists at the
RIA NOVOSTI agency on 13 October. He explained
his thinking by saying that it is no use formulating our
Strategic goals and tasks in a hurry, as certain State
Duma deputies are proposing. Realistically, the basic
provisions of such a document could be formulated over
the next five years.

Yu. Baturin named among the main problems that con-
cern Russia’s top political leadership today the indica-
tions of a crisis in relations with the West, NATO’s
eastward expansion plans, the country’s defense capa-
bility, economic crime, and the Islamic factor. As for the
first two problems, according to the adviser, much will
be clarified as a result of B. Yeltsin’s upcoming visit
to New York and his contacts with leaders of Western
countries, and also at the Conference on Nuclear Secu-
rity which is to take place in Moscow next year.

Dwelling on the problem of defense capability, Yu.
Baturin drew attention to the fact that there is still no
certainty on the main issue: How big an army do we
need — 2 million men or 1.7 million? In his opinion
we must save the unique production facilities that could
collapse due to underfinancing. It would then take
decades to restore them. As for the numerical strength
of the army, even if the policy of reducing it prevails,
mobilization could be carried out very rapidly should
the need arise.

Economic crime — that is the most real threat to
national security today, the president’s adviser went
on. Some 30,000 enterprises under different forms
of ownership are controlled by criminal associations.
Bribed civil servants provide assistance to one in every
four criminal groups. Bribes sometimes run to hundreds
of thousands of dollars. There is no all-purpose means
of combating this evil, just as there is no money for
combating crime. In this situation, Yu. Baturin noted,
we could make use of the experience of certain foreign

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 41

countries to expose those who take bribes, namely: not
only look for the illegal sources of their income, but also
work backward, i.e. from how much a person spends. If,
say, a Civil servant with a miserly wage builds a luxury
cottage, the tax police and the special service should
take an interest in him. Asked what size of bribes have
been offered to him personally, Yu. Baturin replied: "I
am happy to say that nobody has offered me anything
yet."

The Islamic factor as a threat to national security is
viewed by the authorities in the light of their complex
relationships with certain Muslim countries. It also
played a part in the Chechen crisis — Dudayev was
given support by several such countries in the form
of money, weapons, and fighters. Nonetheless there is
no religious or ethnic principle underlying this crisis.
As for the problem of a political settlement of the
Chechen crisis, Yu. Baturin stressed, D. Dudayev cannot
be excluded from the talks process.

Gaydanov’s Program To Get Procuracy ‘Out of
Crisis’

LD1210124295

{[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow NTV in Russian at
2120 GMT on 11 October on its "Versions" program
Carries a recorded interview with Oleg Gaydanov, acting
Russian prosecutor general, by Sergey Dorenko, place
and date not given, and carries comments from various

Gaydanov as a permanent candidate for the post. The

Presenter Sergey Dorenko notes that if Gaydanov could
solve one of the country’s high-profile murder cases
in the run-up to the elections, then he would earn the
gratitude of Yeltsin and his government.

Gaydanov begins by saying: "An operation was carried
out in Moscow, but we did not say that it was being
Carried out as part of the Listyev case on the strength
of specific circumstances. During the course of one
day we conducted 100 searches in Moscow, using a
considerable number of forces, and we hoped, we were
convinced, that we would detain people who would have
charges brought against them, but unfortunately it turned
out that when procuracy investigators and operational
staff arrived at a number of flats intending to arrest
people, they found no one there. It was clear that there
had been a betrayal." Gaydanov goes on to say that he
has asked both Interior Minister Anatoliy Kulikov and
Mikhail Barsukov, head of the Federal Security Service,
to investigate this.

Asked whether society can be expected to believe
in the existence of justice in Russia when so many

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

42 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

murder cases remained unsoived, Gaydanov replies:
"Unfortunately there is neither sense nor any point in
denying your allegation. The only thing I can say is that
everything is not as bad as it may seem from what you
have just said. I am citing these figures from memory
rather than reading them from a paper, but this year
alone procuracy investigators sent to court more than
25,000 criminal cases concerning murderers, and the
average rate of cases of premeditated murder solved in
Russia is 75 percent; that is, 75 out of 100 murders are
solved." Gaydanov notes that this compares favorably
with European figures. "But," he continues, “we are
very concerned at present at the fact that the rate of
premeditated murders solved in the Moscow area is
approximately 40 percent or slightly lower." The rate at
which contract killings were solved was “unacceptably
low", Gaydanov says. He ascribes this to the economic
situation in the country and the modern psychology of
Russian citizens.

To remedy the situation the acting prosecutor general
says it is most important to stop the departure of compe-
tent, committed professionals from the law-enforcement
bodies. He said that "quite a lot" of people had left over
the past four years but by no means everyone. In order
to stop the flow of people, “sacrifices are needed. What
kind of sacrifices? Not in the physical sense, but sac-
rifices by society and the state in the economic sense,
the material and technical sense, the financing of this
sphere of state activity." This would be mentioned in an
appeal being prepared for the Federation Council and
State Duma.

What worried the procuracy, Gaydanov says, was not
crime in general but the professionalism of organized
crime and the fact that "criminals have crept in amongst
deputies, into politics, into the state structure; this is the
most terrible thing. Even money will not help in this
regard tomorrow; the goodwill of society as a whole is
needed. Society, and of course the government carrying
out the will of society, must set aside some kind of
social programs, but we’ll assume a limit of course, and
transfer resources, financial and human, to this area."

after all that happened. But these times are clearly such,
a time of troubles in Russia, that people like that who are
two-faced, who serve the interests of the strong, always
come to the top, especially at this time of troubles. And

of course he has patrons behind him in whose interests

Filatov Interviewed on Debate Over ‘Forming’
Upper House

MK1410130995 Moscow MOSKOVSKIY
KOMSOMOLETS in Russian 10 Oct 95 p2

{Interview with Sergey Filatov, presidential chief of
staff, answering MOSKOVSKIY KOMSOMOLETS’
‘Question of the Week:’ "Can a Russian Federation
Citizen Do Without the Federation Council?"}

(FBIS Translated Text] (MOSKOVSKIY KOMSOMO-
LETS] Is a compromise between the president's and the
Duma’s versions of the law on forming (electing) the
Federation Council possible?

[Filatov] The most important thing is clear: Generally
speaking, elections for the State Duma make no sense
without a Federation Council.

The issue is a matter of principle. In this regard it is
probably impossible to find a compromise because the
Constitution stipulates: The Federation Council is to
be "formed" from representatives of the executive and
legislative branches of power. The State Duma, for its
part, is to be “elected.” These are two different terms —
“elected” and "formed." If one article of the Constitution
uses two different words, it is fairly obvious that they
have different meanings. Now as for the representation
itself. The Fundamental Law says: “one representative
from each executive and legislative branch of power." I
think the Constitutional Court’s interpretation will help
us understand the precise purpose of this article, but
“one representative each" means that only the executive
body and the legislature can decide whom they should
delegate to work on the Federation Council. This
representative in fact pursues the interests that are put
into practice by the legislative and executive bodies of
“his” Federation component. The State Duma’s version

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

says: These representatives should be elected by the
people on the recommendation of the executive and
legislative bodies. This means that they will no longer
represent the executive and legislative branches of
power, but the component itself, that is to say, the
people. This makes a considerable difference: We have
often had cases when having received or confirmed
one’s authority by popular vote, they in fact do not
pursue the line that is adopted on the ground in the
Federation component itself.

What is the idea behind the Federation Council? We
have 89 Federation components. There was a time when
we managed to agree upon laws that applied to all
Federation components with 40 of them at best. Under
the current Constitution, all 89 components, who have
representatives of both their legislative and executive
bodies on the council, must say if the law in question
is good or bad for them, if they need this law or not.
The main objective is to assess what the Duma has
passed, naturally, accounting for the fact that the Duma
is formed mostly according to the partisan principle.
This is how clear-cut, direct communications between a
Federation component, the federal parliament, and the
Russian Federation president are exercised. If we opt,
let’s say, for electing the chamber "by the people," we
will lose the connection between the central authorities
of the components and the federal level.

[MOSKOVSKIY KOMSOMOLETS] What is bad, in
your view, from the point of view of internal political
complications, about forming the Federation Council
without elections, by presidential edict, or extending the
incumbent Federation Council’s term of office pending
the definitive creation of a legislative framework?

[Filatov] How will developments evolve? I think that
since the Constitutional Court has accepted this request
for consideration, it will give its interpretation of the
Constitution on this matter, and there is hope that in
accordance with this clarification the State Duma will
adopt a law that will be in line with the Constitution
and will not have to be sent to the Constitutional Court
right away, regardless of the Duma’s overriding the
presidential veto.

We have considered all these options. It seems that there
are three main options here. I mean not our options,
these are the options of the legal elite who analyzed
the situation proceeding from the fact that we may in
fact end up with a nonworking Federal Assembly. Law
students have suggested moves that could be taken with
the least, let’s say, losses in legal terms. The best option
would be to pass the law. It seems doubtful that, as is
sometimes said, passing a law within two months would
be impossible. We know that yesterday (6 October) the

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 43

State Duma yet again passed a law in three readings at
once.

The first option is the simplest one: The president or
a law extends the Federation Council’s term of office
starting 12 December. The operating practice of any
state agency shows that it does not cease to exist be-
fore it turns its business over to its successor or to an-
other generation for the purpose of continuity. In fact,
the Constitution does not call for the second convoca-
tion of the Federation Council. There was only the first
one. Subsequently it should be formed as if regardless
of dates, as the legislative and executive authorities in
the Federation components are reelected. Naturally, the
problem arises of replacing a delegate with one who
is authorized to represent the component in the Fed-
eration Council. This can happen repeatedly within a
year because there is no convocation for the Federation
Council as such. But what is bad about this option is the
fact that it will not facilitate lawmaking work owing to
the constant turnover. Generally speaking, this can con-
tinue for a long time to come, this is one thing. Another
thing is that representatives of the components will not
be able to take their seats in the Federation Council be-
fore the law is passed in order to start representing their
Federation component at the federal level. Third, there
is no direct reference in the Constitution to the Fed-
eration Council’s operation on a continuing basis until
a new one is formed — one can only draw on indi-
rect constitutional references. The second option boils
down to forming the Federation components by presi-
dential edict, drawing on the direct action of the Consti-
tution. But the vulnerable spot here is that the Consti-
tution says “from legislative and executive authorities,"
which means that they themselves appoint their repre-
sentatives. For this reason the president can either invite
them to nominate someone to the Federation Council
or himself appoint certain officials, let’s say, executive
branch heads and chairmen of legislative bodies. On the
whole, there are deviations here that may give grounds
for turning to the Constitutional Court and questioning
the Federation Council’s legitimacy. As for the third op-
tion. Since the article of the Constitution stipulates that
it is legislative and executive bodies that must make the
appointments, they could themselves determine who can
represent them on the Federation Council without wait-
ing for the law. In this case it would be easier to form
the Federation Council itself from among already exist-
ing representatives by presidential edict.

[MOSKOVSKIY KOMSOMOLETS] Is a situation pos-
sible where the deputies would deliberately drag their
feet on the law so as to force the president to issue an
edict and to accuse the head of state of unconstitutional
actions?

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

44 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

[Filatov] I do not rule out the possibility that there are
political forces wishing to exploit that. You see, the
more material on deviations from the Constitution or
overstepping one’s powers it accumulates, the better
off, naturally, the opposition will be. Then there will be
the impression that legislation is being violated at the
very top — although we all realize that the opposition
itself often prods us to take such steps if only because
many laws are still nonexistent. Generally speaking, our
legislative framework is riddled with gaps; moreover,
the laws already adopted are often at variance with one
another and even with the Constitution. For this reason
this cannot and should not be ruled out, but I think that
ways out should be sought. It is not by chance that Boris
Nikolayevich was the first to take such a step and was
the first to go to the Constitutional Court in order to
help the deputies find the right solution.

([MOSKOVSKIY KOMSOMOLETS] When the text of
the Constitution was being drawn up, was the upper
chamber of the Federal Assembly conceived as a
directly elected one?

[Filatov] No, it wasn’t. Moreover, I can tell you that
literally a few days before Yeltsin signed the text to
press, the words “head of the executive branch" and
“head of the legislative branch of power" were removed
from the draft Constitution. It was specified in abso-
lutely clear terms there that it is they who shall represent
their Federation components on the Federation Council.
These words were removed because constitutional re-
form in the Federation components was announced and
the process of forming the structures of authority began.
It was not similar everywhere: In certain components
the executive branch was headed by the presidents; in
others, by the heads of the republics; in still others, by
the chiefs of the State Councils, and so forth. For this
reason, SO as not to create confusion and so that it will
be su tly easier to sort it out we removed these
words from the draft Constitution. Had they been re-
tained, there would have been no argument now and,
maybe, there would have even been no need to pass
the law because everything would have been clearly
defined in the Constitution. Presently, however, natu-
tal questions arise: Who should be a representative, and
how should the representative be appointed? Nonethe-
less, the “how" is defined by the Constitution, namely,
by decision of the executive or legislative authorities of
a Russian Federation component.

16 October 1995

LD1410144395 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1322 GMT 14 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 14 (INTER-
FAX) — Russia’s Defense Ministry has decided to re-
inforce its group of troops in North Caucasus.

As a military source has disclosed, Colonel General
Viadimir Semenov will fly to the North Caucasian
Military District on October 24 on a Defense Minister
Pavel Grachev’s instruction.

“The general plans to see on the spot whether the district
is complete with troops and arms because of plans to
elevate its status (completeness must be no less than
70%)," the source reported.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 45

“The military leadership pays special attention to the
district because of the grave situation in Chechnya and
instability in North Caucasus as a whole,” the source
explained.

Talking to an INTERFAX reporter, Semenov said that
his trip to the district was not "extraordinary by nature."
He said he regularly inspected troops. For this reason,
he added, there is no need “to create a sensation over
my trip to North Caucasus."

Semenov reported that the North Caucasus Military
District was now manned better than other districts.
However, he admitted that the military leadership paid
special attention to this district above all because “of
the complex situation in North Caucasus."

Manufacturer Advertises MiG Fighters to NATO
Countries

MK1410091595 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian
11 Oct 95 p4

{Report by Anna Andrianova: “Chances That MiG
Fighters Will Be Ordered by NATO Countries Are
Slim. ‘MAPO-MiG’s’ Advertising Campaign Has Not
Brought Any Results Yet")

{[FBIS Translated Text] An official representing the
press service of "MAPO-MiG [Moscow Aviation
Production Association—Mikoyan and Gurevich]," a
production association manufacturing Russian fighter
planes, has said that, despite the fact that the FRG’s
military agency has recognized Russian MiG-29 fight-
ers as superior to the U.S. F-16 and F-4F and the
French Mirage-2000, no new contracts for the sale of

MiG’s are expected.

According to information made available by "MAPO-
MiG’s" press center, the German Air Force voiced a
preference for the Russian fighters after routine NATO
air force exercises in which the MiG-29 won all air
battles with U.S. and French fighters. It will be recalled
that the German Air Force is armed with 24 combat and
training MiG-29 fighters, inherited by the FRG from the
former GDR’s Air Force. Quite probably, these fighters
will take part in NATO’s military operations in Bosnia
if hostilities there are resumed.

The dissemination of information about foreign experts’
high opinion of Russian MiG’s and the publication of
such materials in Western media are regarded by some
observers as an advertising campaign conducted by
"MAPO-MiG.” Incidentally, this production association
is not denying the fact. As is known, "“MAPO-MiG"
is one of the few Russian defense industry enterprises
given the right to export its products independently, not

pk ob Deeg ogy Material. Copying and dissemination inati
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

46 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

via "Rosvooruzheniye." Thus, the advertising campaign
seems quite logical.

Despite the high appraisals, such as by the German
Air Force, and the efforts of the Russian manufacturer
and special exporter, chances that in the near future
orders for the delivery of MiG’s will be received from
NATO countries are very slim, for political reasons.
Therefore, most probably, third countries — India,
Malaysia, etc. — will continue to remain "MAPO-
MiG’s” main foreign customers.

It will be recalled that "MAPO-MiG’s” latest deal was
the sale of eight MiG-29 fighters to Slovakia, estimated
at $200 million. Before that, last year, Slovakia had
purchased five such fighters, worth $150 million. In both
cases, deliveries were made against the settlement of
Russia's external debt.

Federal Assembly

Duma Spurns Premier’s Olive Branch on Budget

MK1410150495 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY
in Russian 14 Oct 95 pp 1-2

[Report by Boris Boyko under the "Budgetary Process"
rubric: “From the Molotov-Ribbentrop Pact to the
Chernomyrdin-Rybkin Pact")

(FBIS Translated Text) After a fortnight’s preliminary
clashes between parliamentarians and the government
over the 1996 draft budget, the budgetary process was
finally given the official go-ahead yesterday. After the
traditional reports — a “political” report by the prime
minister and an “economic” one by the finance minister
— the State Duma meeting discussed the draft in its
first reading. Despite peacemaking statements by the
premier, who proposed that "a nonaggression pact, or
— better yet — a pact on peaceful coexistence” be
signed with the State Duma, the parliament's attitude
to the document did not change and remained hostile:
No faction proposed that the budget be approved. For
this reason even the fact that no decision at all was made
as a result can be seen as a considerable achievement.

Premier: To Proceed From Reality, Not From
Wishful Thinking

The prime minister’s report on the socioeconomic
situation in the country in the first nine months of
the year was detailed and, according to its concept,
intended to have a hypnotic influence on the deputies
on the eve of the upcoming budgetary battles. At
the same time Viktor Chernomyrdin made a point of
emphasizing his love for peace from the outset, stressing
that the decision to brief the deputies on the emerging
economic situation will enable them “not only to analyze
jointly the economic situation, but also to account for
the Duma’s viewpoint in current government policy."
Calling on deputies to be balanced in their judgment
("while assessing the situation, it is necessary to proceed
from reality, not from wishful thinking, comparing what
we had and what we have achieved"), the prime minister
paid particular attention in his speech to the positive
trends.

In short, the premier sees such trends in the following
areas. The scale of this year’s production slump turned
out to be much smaller than last year’s and even
smaller than forecast. In the first nine months GDP
shrank by only 3 percent as compared to the same
period last year, while in 1994 it fell 12 percent. In
September of this year the overall production volume
remained unchanged against last September's, while
a number of sectors even registered growth. As for

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

prices, their growth has been slowing down with every
passing month — from 18 percent in January to
4.5 percent in September. Noting that in October
inflation will not exceed 5 percent, Chernomyrdin urged
everyone io realize that "the economy is not just
pulling through — it is beginning to live by market
laws." In the premier’s words, a 17-percent reduction
in capital investment compared to last year should not
be seen only in black and white — in the meantime,
housing construction increased by 13 percent during this
period. What the premier mentioned as an important
lesson of the past nine months was the fact that the
noninflationary mechanisms of financing the budget
deficit started operating fairly effectively; regarding
the banking system, he said in a rather metaphorical
fashion that “it is convalescing, removing the dross
of currency speculation from its body." Dwelling in
detail on the government’s objectives, ranging from
the repayment of delayed wages to assistance for the
countryside, the premier noted that, first, it is necessary
to “consolidate and stabilize the positive trends of
economic development” and, second, "to disentangle the
key knots that cause instability.”

Having thus put up the requisite propaganda support
for the government's plans, the premier proceeded to
present the budget. In his words, the philosophy of the
budget entails continuity in politics and the retention of
the current pace of change: “Otherwise we will stumble
once again and trample underfoot everything positive
and reasonable that has already been made.” At the same
time, what is much more important is that Chernomyrdin
admitted that “the draft budget is not dogma” and that
the government is ready for compromise.

Still, it followed from Finance Minister Vladimir Pan-
skov’s speech that the government is, of course, ready
for compromises, but only ones that do not jeopardize
its key macroeconomic targets, particularly, its forecast
of an average monthly inflation rate of 1.2 percent. Pan-
skov reiterated yet again that the 3- to 5-percent level
proposed by the deputies will only fuel the growth of
the budget deficit and inflation. Nonetheless, the finance
minister expressed a readiness to start "a constructive
dialogue” with the State Duma.

Budget Committee: Turn It Down Without
Rejecting It

The government’s peaceloving initiatives were not re-
ciprocated by the deputies, however. Not only did
Mikhail Zadornov, chairman of the Duma’s Budget
Committee, level harsh criticism at the budget, but he
moreover effectively distanced himself from his own
committee’s decision. It should be recalled that a few
days ago Viadimir Panskov said that the Duma Bud-

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 47

get Committee decided in its draft resolution against
rejecting the draft budget, but called for a conciliation
commission to be set up without passing any verdict on
it. This viewpoint appeared to suit the government. But
upon making the committee’s opinion public, Zadornov
proposed — this time on behalf of his "Yabloko" fac-
tion — that a different draft resolution be passed, under
which the budget must be first rejected (this is precisely
what the law on the procedure for considering the bud-
get provides for), and only then would the conciliation
commission be created.

As for the committee’s main complaints about the draft
budget (a number of these complaints were also repeated
by representatives of Duma factions in their statements),
they are as follows. The committee’s main thesis is
simple: The 1996 draft budget does not differ in any way
from the 1995 budget. The underestimated inflation rate
forecast will once again generate a surplus of revenues
that will nonetheless be devalued by a faster pace of
actual inflation. Therefore. real revenues will prove to
be lower. Zadornov supported this thought with a fairly
trite argument to the effect that financial stabilization in
the country is being achieved at the expense of a drastic
reduction in expenditures on all key budget items.
Moreover, the committee is opposed to government-
proposed changes to the tax legislation; thinks that the
expected proceeds from privatization are overestimated
(in the committee’s view, it will be impossible to earn
12 trillion rubles), while social expenditures are set
too low (the government thinks, however, that their
share will increase); and also doubted the correctness
of the government-proposed methodology of calculating
transfers from the federal budget to the Federation
components’ budgets.

Khachim Karmokov, chairman of the Counting Cham-
ber, who took the floor after Zadornov, stunned the par-
liamentarians with the statement that the foreign debt,
which grew from 25 trillion to 50 trillion rubles com-
pared to the 1995 budget (a number of foreign debt
payments are due next year), may exceed the size of
the budget deficit.

An Open Final

Representatives of nearly all factions echoed the Budget
Committee’s view. The leitmotiv of virtually all staie-
ments was the assertion that the 1996 budget does not
differ from the 1995 budget in any way, while given
all the negative consequences of an austere policy one
can no longer trust the government or subscribe to the
continuation of the same policy. The "Yabloko,” Com-
munist Party of the Russian Federation, and Democratic
Party of Russia factions leveled the most scathing crit-
icism at the government budget. Oksana Dmitriyeva,

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owncrs.

48 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

member of the "Yabloko" faction, proposed a number
of budget policy alternatives, but doubted the current
cabinet’s ability to realize them. Anatoliy Gordeyev,
member of the Communist faction, said that the gov-
ernment is following the worst traditions of the former
Gosplan [State Planning Committee] of “first seeking
low plan targets and then exceeding them.” Naturally,
the factions insisted on rejecting the budget. The other
factions, although they echoed the criticism, called for
a conciliation commission to be set up as soon as pos-
sible and pointed out that the budget must be adopted
by the current State Duma. Their arguments were not in
the government’s favor all the same: "One should not
give the government a free hand.” The sole faction that
agreed with the need to continue the austerity line pro-
vided for in the draft budget turned out to be "Russia’s
Choice" — Yegor Gaydar, its leader, even launched into
a polemic with "Yabloko,” flatly rejecting its proposal
on paying off the budget deficit by issuing more money
and credits: In his view, this would boost the deficit,
which “is already set in the draft budget at an imper-
missibly high level."

To all appearances, the deputies were so tired after the
lengthy debate that they never adopted a definite deci-
sion. The proposal to reject the budget was supported
by 180 deputies — and the one that a conciliation com-
mission be set up without turning the draft down, by
143. As a result, the final vote was postponed until next
week.

[Article includes the following boxed commentary by
the Political Department]

Viktor Chernomyrdin has changed his style of commu-
nicating with State Duma deputies. His statement yes-
terday was imbued with the desire to avoid yet another
confrontation by fair means or foul, while instead of the
usual metal (with which he used to pronounce his trade-
mark phrase "don’t interfere in the ministers’ work")
there were conciliatory tones in his voice. Citing the
complexity of the transition period, when “the interests
of the electoral struggle run counter to economic policy
objectives," Chernomyrdin called on the deputies not
to carry the political struggle into relations between the
branches of power and urged the Duma to sign "a nonag-
gression pact for a period until the December elections.”
After these words, a budget was submitted to the Duma
that was in for a curious fate. No coherent decision on
the bill could be made: It was neither rejected, nor sent
to a conciliation commission. The only thing the Duma
approved was the Budget Committee’s proposal that a
draft resoiution be drawn up by the next meeting.

This is puzzling. A majority of those who were present
at the Duma — deputies, government members, journal-

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

ists — expected the budget to be unequivocally spurned.
But only 180 deputies voted to reject (out of the requi-
site 226), among whom there were no Zhirinovskiyites,
Gaydarites, or members of the "Stability" and "Rossiya"
groups. This impasse can be attributed, perhaps, only to
the approaching elections. Agrarian Party leader Lap-
shin straightforwardly likened the premier’s speech to
“an electoral advertisement of the NDR [‘Russia Is
Our Home’) electoral movement,” but at the same time
pointed out with satisfaction that the government is be-
ginning to face up to the countryside’s problems. It is
also noteworthy that the premier thought it necessary to
dwell on the nationalities issue in his report devoted to
the economy, calling for the diversity and uniqueness
of peoples living on Russian soil to be taken into ac-
count when tackling this problem so as completely to
rule out ethnic conflicts. It should be recalled that his
NDR pins its main hopes precisely on ethnic republics,
where it expects to win the highest percentage of the
vote. The premier’s promise to increase future spending
on the army and the military-industrial sector did not go
unnoticed either. In a word, the premier is bracing for
elections and is adopting a corresponding line of con-
duct. So are the deputies. Having finally realized that at
this stage any vigorous moves against the prime minister
can only improve his electoral chances, they did not un-
equivocally reject the budget. But nor can they embrace
his call for the hatchet to be buried and the budget tc be
passed on the eve of a decisive showdown. It seems that
the room for maneuver has narrowed to the limit, and
the war between the premier and the deputies is taking
on the nature of sluggish, positional warfare.

Draft Budget Spells ‘Catastrophe’ for Army
MM1310134495 Moscow KRASNAYA ZVEZDA
in Russian 12 Oct 95 pl

[Article by Vladimir Yermolin: "Budget for 1996 Does
Not Meet Defense Needs. To Adopt It Would Be To
Push the Army Into the Abyss"}

{[FBIS Translated Text] On Monday the State Duma
Defense Committee held an enlarged session with the
participation of Andrey Kokoshin, Russian Federation
first deputy defense minister, Colonel General Ana-
toliy Solomatin, deputy defense minister and chief of
construction and billeting of troops, Colonel General
Vladimir Churanov, deputy defense minister and chief
of the Russian Federation Armed Forces Rear Services,
Colonel General Anatoliy Sitnov, chief of armaments of
the Russian Federation Armed Forces, Lieutenant Gen-
eral Vasiliy Kuznetsov, first deputy chief of the Military
Budget and Funding Main Directorate, and also repre-
sentatives of the Finance Ministry, Ministry of Econom-
ics, Ministry of Atomic Energy, and State Committee

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

for Defense Industry. The implementation of the 1995
defense budget and the draft defense budget for 1996
were discussed.

The session was closed, but in practice the essence of all
the speeches boiled down to the same point: Unless the
approach toward funding the Russian Federation Armed
Forces is cardinally changed, next year will be a year
of irreversible disintegration processes in the Army and
Navy, the defense industry, and essentially the state’s
security system.

What are we seeing for the fourth year now during
formation of the federal budget? The Defense Ministry
prepares a detailed case for expenditure on maintaining
the Armed Forces, on arms purchases, on military
science, on capital construction, and on the payment
of pensions to servicemen, gives the ultimate total
for the necessary expenditure, and passes all this to
the government, which eventually cuts the requested
sum by at least 50 percent. The cuts are usually
made so swiftly that you cannot help doubting the
validity of the government’s calculations. It all looks
more like a purely mechanical operation to tailor the
defense budget to the procrustean bed of the budget
deficit. Furthermore, it transpires that the government
unhesitatingly understates the anticipated inflation rate.
This produces a kind of reserve fund of income which
may give the government room for maneuver, but the
first people to pay for this policy are budget-funded
workers, including servicemen.

The past year has been a vivid illustration of this. Not
only was the level of inflation for 1995 on which finan-
cial thinkers based the budget out of step with the actual
figure, but entirely legally validated increases in pay, the
war in Chechnya, and the full amount of last year’s debts
were also “left out of account." Typically, the Army it-
self is blamed for these “extras” on top of the expen-
diture headings of the defense budget. It is argued that
pay should not have been increased if the budget made
no provision for this expansive gesture. However, the
Army never acts arbitrarily. All the increases stem from
Russian presidential edicts and State Duma decisions.
Moreover, contrary to the law, the following practice
has been introduced: Wages are increased for civilian
budget-funded workers, while the military are told to
await this joyous event on the sidelines. Tens of thou-
sands of officers and contract personnel are currently
serving without having received their pay for August-
September. The payment delays are snowballing. Fi-
nance Ministry representative Andrey Astakhov, who
addressed the session, gave an assurance that, in accor-
dance with a Russian Federation presidential edict, the
Armed Forces will receive before the end of the year
as a matter of priority extra payments to cover expen-

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS )

diture on food and pay. In other words, it is a question
of exceptional measures — emergency measures, so to
speak. Although everything was calculated a year ago
and it would have been sufficient merely to base the
budget on real figures — real figures, not wishful think-
ing.

As a result of this practice the Armed Forces are today
at the end of their tether. I will quote a number of points
from the State Duma Defense Committee’s findings on
the draft law "On the Federal Budget for 1996": "For the
first time in the last four years debts in respect of expen-
diture on maintaining the Armed Forces have reached
the level of quarterly spending, despite the fact that the
Russian Federation Government has allocated appropri-
ations for these purposes in the amounts planned in the
budget (61.6 percent). Furthermore, delays in issuing
pay to servicemen have reached a month or more, the
money for food supplies planned for the year has been
totally spent, and payment defaults in respect of mu-
nicipal services and other operational spending are 50
percent greater than the annual level of appropriations
for these purposes."

What does that mean — the money for food supplies
has been totally spent? It means that emergency rations
are now being consumed. It means that money is being
taken from other, already meagerly funded categories
of expenditure and used for food. Few Russians uncon-
nected with military service realize that TODAY THE
ONLY TASK RESOLVED BY THE ARMY IS HOW
TO FEED SOLDIERS. How to clothe them and provide
footwear are now secondary problems. Stores have not
been replenished for years.

In 1995 rear services specialists calculated how many
pairs of boots and sets of uniform and what quantity
of food according to existing norms and of fuel will be
needed for an army of 1.5 million. They gave the lowest
possible sum as 12 trillion rubles [R]. The motherland
(in the shape of the government!) allocated R5.7 trillion,
but the sum that actually reached Army coffers was
even less than that. Those are the figures. What lies
behind them is people. Defenders of the fatherland who
are being denied proper food and warm clothing by
that fatherland. And that assertion is in no way an
exaggeration, it is a simple statement of fact. It is a
question of something basic — the health of the people
whom the state has drafted for military service. And
what about purchases of arms and the renewal of the
pool of equipment and arsenals of weapons? During
the current year this category of expenditure has been
funded to the tune of only 39.8 percent.

Incidentally, the U.S. President is allocating $25 billion
for a technological breakthrough to leave other countries

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

50 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

(not least Russia) behind in the arms sphere. Whereas
given the present attitude toward defense, according
to specialists, by 2005 we will have one and a half
motorized infantry divisions with new equipment, a
few missile units, and five submarines (the rest will
be unable to fly, sail, or shoot). In the words of one
general, “today the Army is being disarmed at a much
faster pace than in 1941." The facts are clear, and the
figures speak for themselves. The military are taking
them and knocking on the door of the highest offices.

It is already clear even to laymen that the Russian
Armed Forces are entering a black period of catastrophe,
yet the government is again proposing a draft budget in
which the expenditure on national defense requested by
the military department is virtually halved. Once again
we are being told to believe in only modest growth in
inflation, which is manifestly sheer fantasy. Once again
the full amount of the debts for 1994-1995 is being
ignored. Once again we are being told to issue soldiers
with one boot each and to feed them every other day.
The State Duma Defense Committee’s findings say in
this connection: "The expenditure on national defense
envisaged in the draft budget is below the 1995 level.
While it is proposed to increase general federal spending
by 70 percent in comparisun with 1995, it is planned to
increase defense spending by only 55 percent. This fails
to take account of the virtually inevitable increase of at
least R12 trillion in defense expenditure in 1995."

As a result, they declare, the State Duma Defense
Committee will vote against adopting the draft law "On
the Federal Budget for 1996" during the first reading.
The version of the draft law prepared by the committee
envisages increasing expenditure on national defense to
R110.925 trillion. Meanwhile, the military are pressing
for R134 trillion, on the grounds that this sum is
the result of direct calculation while Defense Ministry
financial workers proceeded on the basis of minimum
requirements.

There is still time to coordinate positions. The Defense
Committee, according to Aleksandr Piskunov, besides
seeking the rejection of the draft law during the first
reading, will seek to adjust the budget on the basis
of a computed assumed inflation index of 3.5, to cut
paramilitary structures under other departments (17
ministries and departments currently have their own
troops with 3.9 million people in their ranks!), to man
only the Armed Forces and Border Troops with draftees,
and to fully protect all articles of the defense budget
(the proposal was submitted at the session by Finance
Ministry representative Andrey Astakhov).

So a decisive stage in the fate of the Russian Army is
beginning — the struggle for a realistic defense budget

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

in 1996. In the event of failure, it will scarcely be
necessary to establish a defense budget for 1997. What
is the point in maintaining an Army that has lost the
capacity to defend the country?

Duma Concessions on Upper Hous ' Law Seen as
‘Sham’

MM1310130395 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI

in Russian 13 Oct 95 pl

{Article by Olga Burkaleva and Lyubov Tsukanova:
“The Law on the Federation Council: Concessions or
Countermoves?"]

(FBIS Translated Text] Work on the law on the for-
mation of the Federation Council has exposed dramatic
differences in perception of the problem. And not only
between the head of state and parliament as a whole, but
also among the legislators themselves. After the Russian
Federation president vetoed the law adopted by the Fed-
eral Assembly, the danger arose that parliament would
not “find time” for the law by December, that is, by the
time the powers of the present Federation Council ex-
pire. The fear that the president would form the upper
chamber by edict made the State Duma accept a com-
promise and abandon the principle of direct elections to
the Federation Council, which it had formerly upheld.
But a compromise was also accepted by the president,
who had an interest in the law being adopted in the
end so that the upper chamber of parliament would be
formed on an irreproachably constitutional basis. The
presidential side’s compromise was that the new version
of the draft law submitted to the Duma did not stipu-
late that a region’s interests must be represented in the
Federation Council specifically by the region’s “number
one." The earlier proposal that the chamber should in-
clude three or four representatives of the federal organs
of power was dropped. Thus, mutual concessions pro-
vided the opportunity to draw up a compromise version
of the law.

However, it would be premature to say that the new law
just adopted by the State Duma has finally removed the
conflict. The amendments to the “presidential” version
of the law submitted by the Duma Committee on
Legislation and adopted by the Duma satisfy, on the one
hand, the president’s wish to form the upper chamber
from among leaders of the legislative and executive
power of the Russian Federation components. But on the
other hand they bar the path to the Federation Council
for heads of administrations who are not elected by
the population but appointed by the president. Today
there are 54 of these in the Russian Federation. That
means that under the new law more than half the heads
of administrations will not be able, at the outset, to
participate in the work of the second Federation Council.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

The participants in September’s conference of heads of
representative organs of power of the Russian Federa-
tion components did not, admittedly, see this version of
the law as a disaster. The Federation Council, they be-
lieve, will be able to start work, since a quorum (more
than two-thirds of the membership) is already assured;
the only thing the upper chamber will not be able to
do is to adopt constitutional federal laws. And in time
the other governors will be “brought in" as the elections
proceed.

However, it is doubtful whether this version will suit
the president. First, it means that for a long time the
executive power’s representation on the new upper
chamber will be weakened. Second, the law overrides
the presidential edict of 17 September 1995 insofar as
it recommends postponing the elections of heads of
administrations to December 1996: The law states that
the elections must be set within eight months.

But the "Isakov committee’s" amendments go even fur-
ther. The version adopted stipulates that any decisions
on extending the powers of deputies of legislative or-
gans of Russian Federation components have no legal
force. At the same time the same 17 September edict
recommends the legislative organs of Russian Federa-
tion components, whose terms expire, by and large, in
1996, to postpone the elections to December 1997. As
Sergey Filatov, chief of the Russian Federation Presi-
dential Staff, announced recently, such decisions have
already been adopted in 47 regions of the country. In
practice this means that if the law is adopted the Fed-
eration Council will be short of at least a further 50
members in 1996-1997. Since this means in practice that
the Federation Council will simply be unable to operate
with this composition, the main aim of the authors of
the amendments to the law becomes obvious — to an-
nul the operation of the Russian Federation president’s
17 September edict. The "concessions" are in fact coun-
termoves.

What are the authors of these amendments hoping for?
Maybe that the president, who has repeatedly declared
the desirability of forming the Federation Council
according to the law on the basis of the time limit,
will agree to the new "Duma" version, and then the
provisions of the said edict on postponing the elections
of heads of administrations and legislative organs in
the Russian Federation components will in effect be
annulled. Objectively, however, a series of election
campaigns in the course of 1996 plays into the hands,
hypothetically, of the communist opposition, which is
counting on victory in the Stated Duma elections and
also — riding that wave — in all the subsequent
elections, as a result of which the Communists and
Agrarians will, by perfectly democratic means, get into

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 51

all the power structures in the regions and the Federation
Council and also, as they clearly hope, the presidency.
Maybe they are overestimating their strength here, but
the idea is quite obvious. It seems the Communists do
not want to repeat the democrats’ mistake in 1991, when
they "missed" the political tide and gradually lost their
electorate.

But the opposition can hardly expect the president
not to see through this calculation on the part of his
political opponents and to agree to the version proposed
by the State Duma. Nor is it certain that the law in
this form will be passed by the Federation Council,
although that is not ruled out. Most likely, time will
be wasted again, and the State Duma will have to revert
to conciliation procedures and seek true — instead of
sham — compromises. But time is short for that.

Duma Discusses Resolutions on Rebuilding
Chechnya

LD1310153695 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1020 GMT 13 Oct 95

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, Oct 13 (INTER-
FAX) — The State Duma carried nearly unanimously 4
resolutions on Chechnya at its plenary session Friday.

Under one of the resolutions the audit chamber will
check the use of the federal funds allocated for rebuild-
ing the Chechen economy and social sphere and for
dealing with the consequences of the armed conflict in
Chechnya.

The audit chamber has a month to report back to
parliament.

Another resolution calls on the president and govern-
ment to take urgent steps to help the citizens wishing to
leave Chechnya, in particular by paying them compen-
sations for loss of homes.

Parliament committees and the Nationalities and Re-
gional Policy Ministries have until November 15 to draft
and submit to the State Duma a bill on aid to those who
wish to leave Chechnya.

Only 1 vote was cast against a resolution moved by Se-
curity Committee Chairman Viktor Ilyukhin, commu-
nist, on improving the safety of people engaged in re-
building Chechnya.

A resolution moved by the Liberal Democratic Party
group that would call for suspension of financing the
rebuilding work in Chechnya until a constitutional order
and federal institutions are restored there.

Kovalev’s moves in the Chechen republic. Kovalev
was appointed Russia’s human rights commissioner in
January, 1993.

the Chechen Republic, which resulted in a replacement
of power,” the premier noted.

swing the course to war.

Kulikov Interviewed on Chechnya

LD1610121895 Moscow NTV in Russian
1800 GMT 15 Oct 95

{Interview with Russian Internal Affairs Minister Ana-
toliy Kulikov by Yevgeniy Kiselev; place and date not
given — recorded]

(FBIS Translated Text] [Kiselev] Anatoliy Sergeyevich,
exactly a week ago, speaking on television, you came
out in favor of the introduction of a state of emergency
in Chechnya after the attack on General [Anatoliy] Ro-
manov. Nevertheless, it was decided after a meeting be-
tween the president and the prime minister on Tuesday
[10 October] not to introduce a state of emergency. Do
you still think that it is necessary to introduce a state of
emergency?

[Kulikov] The arguments which I put forward, and
which the minister of defense put forward, were based
on purely technical military considerations, on how
measures could be taken in as short a time as possible
to normalize the situation. The volume of information
at the disposal of the president, the circumstances he
has to consider, the conclusions to be drawn from this
information by him are much wider and the president
adopted this decision. We are obliged to carry out the
president’s decision.

[Kiselev] So it would be easier for you if all these
measures were taken, but at the same time you take
into account the possible political costs?

[Kulikov] Yes, this is what one can think.

[Kiselev] When you proposed that a state of emergency
should be introduced, there were many who took your

[Salambek] Khadzhiyev to uphold law and order even
in Groznyy.

{[Kulikov] As far as the militia is concerned, from
our point of view, the militia in Groznyy is not
badly equipped perhaps for the exception of some
organizational equipment, special equipment, and there
are already some good results. It cannot be said that
it is idle there. We are handing over federal forces
to the militia, and we are making more and more
use of it. Finally, the bodies of internal affairs of the
Chechen Republic have already begun to carry out
night patrols along with the federal militia, with special
purpose militia detachments. Since there have been
many allegations that the federal forces, the interior
troops, and the armed forces have been violating the
law, we are also bringing in representatives of the
Chechen militia there so that they might defuse the

Situation, act as interpreters and simply witness that

[{Kiselev] Nevertheless, the question arises — a politi-

[Kiselev] Do you mean Dudayev?

{Kulikov) Yes, I mean Dudayev, of course. If

here.

(Kulikov] When we are working directly there, one-to-

serious.

cm high, 20-30 cm wide.

Secret Services’ Complicity Hinted
MK1410081395 Moscow OBSHCHAYA GAZETA
in Russian
No. 41, 12-18 Oct 95 (Signed to press 11 Oct 95) p 1
(Unattributed report: “They Have Long Been Searching,
But Do Not Wish To Find")
(FBIS Translated Text] Shamil Basayev has been pro-

claimed Chechnya’s number one terrorist. He is wanted,
but no one is searching for him. We have learned

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

that FSB [Federal Security Service] field officers in
the Northern Caucasus have been tracing Basayev’s
movements, locating his whereabouts, and reporting on
that through their superiors to the very top, but instead
of a response there is silence and no action whatso-
ever. In the meantime, journalists regularly meet with
Basayev without hindrance. The latest of them was Rus-
lan Yamalov, OBSHCHAYA GAZETA’s photojournal-
ist, who filmed his meeting. While in Shali, Ruslan went
to Aslanbek, the local commandant, and asked him to
arrange a meeting with Basayev. They got into a car and
drove to the outskirts of town. There were no detective-
Story tricks, such as blindfolding or zigzagging along
the roads. Once at the meeting place, a fire was made
and the participants sat down on logs.... The conversa-
tion lasted for more than half an hour. Ruslan took a
few pictures, bid farewell, and departed.

The search for the terrorist is simply being sabotaged
by law-enforcement agencies. To risk such unconcealed
sabotage, you need to have fairly important interests.
We are inclined to agree with the view of officers close
to General Romanov: The Chechen side had nothing
to do with the terrorist act; the perpetrators should be
search for on this side.

One last point: The country’s history and the tragedies
of millions of people remind us how dangerous it is
for all of us to have secret services pursuing their own
secret ends.

Eyewitnesses Tell of Village Airstrikes

LD1510174695 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service
in Russian 1649 GMT 15 Oct 95

{By ITAR-TASS special correspondent Nikolay Zag-
noyko]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Shatoy (Chechen Republic), 15
Oct (ITAR-TASS) — Refugees appeared again this
morning in the village of Shatoy, a name familiar to
millions from reports of the fighting last summer. They
were from the nearby highland village of Kharsenoy,
about 50 km south of Groznyy. The village came under
aerial bombardment yesterday, eyewitnesses told this
ITAR-TASS correspondent. They said six unmarked
aircraft and helicopters each made several bombing
and strafing runs over a period of nearly 20 minutes.
The area around the village was littered with numerous
craters. Houses were damaged by shrapnel and one
house was nearly completely destroyed. There were no
casualties.

“As soon as we heard the roar of aircraft we all rushed
to our cellars. That is where we sat out the bombing,”
ITAR-TASS was told by Umidat, a 50-year-old mother

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

of 10. “After that we left all our possessions and
livestock behind, and walked through the night along
mountain paths toward Shatoy, nearly 20 km away. We
bedded down in the local police station here. There are
four old men and one old woman still in Kharsenoy.
They are not well to make the journey themselves, and
we could not carry them."

"I cannot understand who needs to do this type of
thing,” Yusup Dokhshukalov, the prefect of Shatoy
district, told ITAR-TASS. "This morning we contacted
Yuriy Zhadanov, the commander of the 245th Regiment
which is deployed in the district. We have a very good
relationship with him. The colonel himself was surprised
at what had happened, he says that he tried to find out
about the situation from the headquarters of the Joint
Group of Federal Forces in Groznyy but nothing was
known there either. Peace was just beginning to come
about. People were paid their pensions and benefits
and then this attack happens. I would understand the
necessity of military action if Kharsenoy was a rebel
base, but there are only 30 homes there. People live
under tough conditions and we can hardly help them.
There are no telephones in Shatoy, no electricity, the
bakery and the hospital do not work. We are trying
to persuade the refugees to return home. They need
guarantees, but guarantees can only come about as a
result of peace talks. Planes and bombs do not help
such talks.”

the villages of Drago and Belgatoy on October 14.
As a result, 17 people were killed and 23 wounded.
Incidentally, Udugov initially spoke about an air strike
on the village of Kharsinoy.

Meszaros ‘Shocked’ by Raid on Roshni-Chu

LD1410143495 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1322 GMT 14 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] GROZNYY, Oct 14 (INTER-
FAX) — The head of the OSCE mission in Chechnya
Sandor Meszaros was shocked by what he saw in the
village of Roshni-Chu in Chechnya’s Achkhoi-Martan
District on Friday. He made this statement in an inter-
view with INTERFAX given in Groznyy on Saturday
upon his return from Roshni-Chu.

Meszaros confirmed that the village came under a bomb
strike on October 8. According to the local people, he
said, eight assault planes launched two attacks on the
village; they dropped 12 bombs and fired several dozen
missiles. They also fired from submachine guns.

As Meszaros said, 32 houses in the village were totally
or almost totaliy destroyed; 60 or 62 houses were
damaged. Meszaros quoted the local people as saying
that 28 villagers were killed; about 60 were hospitalized.

I do not know whether there were militants in the village
or not, but such an action cannot be justified, Meszaros
said. According to him, the incident clearly shows that
there is no alternative to negotiations and to compliance
with the agreement on military issues.

Meszaroe. No Alternative to Peace Talks

LD141012289' 2#scow ITAR-TASS in English
1204 GMT 14 es 95

[By Nikolay Zagnoiko]

{FBIS Transcribed Text] MOSCOW, October 14
(ITAR-TASS) — The leader of the Groznyy mission
of the Organisation for Security and Cooperation in
Europe [OSCE], Sandor Meszaros, said at a news
conference that there is no reasonable alternative to
further peace talks in Chechnya.

The developments in the village of Roshni-Chu last
Saturday are another proof of that, Meszaros said,
recounting his recent visit to Roshni-Chu.

He quoted the villagers as saying, stressing that he was
quoting what he was told, that 28 civilians were killed
and 60 wounded in a Russian air raid on Roshni-Chu.

The press centre of the Russian federal troops and the
Defence Ministry denied the raid.

Depicting the destruction which he saw in Roshni-Chu,
Meszaros said he did not know whether there were
Chechen fighters in the village, but the air raid would
not be justifiable even if they were there.

Meszaros said he knew that Russian check-points were
attacked too, with lethal toll, and this may not be
tolerated.

He called on the conflicting parties to show maximal
restraint and willingness to pull out of the conflict by
peaceful means.

Meszaros commented the Friday statement by Chechen
top commander Aslan Maskhadov, who scolded the
Groznyy mission of the OSCE as useless.

The mission will continue to work as it has been
working until now, Meszaros said.

He said the mission’s mandate commits it to help all
sides.
If this task is consistently pursued, one of the sides will
be expectably discontent, he pointed out.
That the OSCE mission is criticised indicates that it
carries on this line well, Meszaros said.

ings, functioning of the Supreme Soviet dissolved by

Basayev: No Elections Until Troops Withdraw

LD1510154195 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1351 GMT 15 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text] GROZNYY, Oct 15 (INTER-
FAX) — Chechen field commander Shamil Basayev
told INTERFAX in an interview that elections in Chech-
nya would not be held “until Russian troops are with-
drawn from its territory.”

"We don’t need elections now. When Russia leaves we
will hold the elections ourselves," Basayev said.

INTERFAX Note: Basayev led the gunmen’s raid on
the town of Budennovsk (Stavropol region) as a result
of which over 100 people died.

Basayev said he believed that until the elections in
Chechnya were held, the republic should be run “by
Dzhokar Dudayev and the government of the Chechen
Republic-Ichkeria which Dudayev formed." Basayev
said Dudayev had appointed Chechen chief negotiator

FBIS-SOV-95-199
16 October 1995

at talks with Russia Khozh-Akhmed Yarikhanov as the
chairman of the Chechen Central Election Commission.

Basayev said: “Chechen political parties and movements
currently have only one task: to obtain Russian troop
withdrawal from the republic." He said he considered
the round-table conference of Chechen political parties
and movements, in whose session in Shali he took part
Saturday, as “absolutely useless." He said he believed
that the round-table conference had been created in
Russia’s interests in order to “split the Chechen people."

Basayev protested the bombardment of the Roshni-Chu
village last Friday. “That’s cynical to bomb villages,
kill women and children after signing an agreement on
military problems,” he said.

Basayev also said he believed that "the OSCE was not
interested in fair settlement of the Chechen crisis from
the very beginning.” He accused the OSCE of siding
with Russia.

When asked about possible introduction of the state
of emergency in Chechnya, Basayev wondered: “What
state of emergency can be discussed while a real war is
being waged here?"

(FBIS Translated Text) The all-Kuzbass protest action
planned for 12 October has taken place. But the all-
Kuzbass strike that was predicted did not take place,

pode ae Feat pr material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

62 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

our correspondent Viktor Kostyukovskiy reports from
Kemerovo. Four mines in southern Kuzbass, one in
Prokopyevsk, and a number of sections at several
enterprises have completely stopped work. Some do
not stand to gain from striking, others have thrown
up their hands in despair and see no point in a strike,
while yet others, for example at several enterprises in
Mezhdurechensk, received their wages for the summer
months literally the day before the planned action.

The protesters’ economic demands are not new —
they relate primarily to wage arrears. Their political
demands are no longer new either: the dismissal of the
government, early presidential elections, and a change
to the reform policy. At some enterprises, known to be
loss-making and to have no future, workers, prompted
by their trade union leaders, are demanding “let the
enterprise work," in other words, ensure funding for
enterprises which are a millstone around the Russian
economy’s frail neck. This is the outcome of the ill-
conceived policy of closing such enterprises down.

In some cities in the oblast there have been rallies of
a clearly marked politicai character with an abundance
of red hammer-and-sickle banners and the appropriate
speeches. The loudest and most strident speakers at
those rallies, however, were not the miners but teachers
and veterans. Aman Tuleyev, chairman of the oblast’s
legislative assembly, took part in one such rally in
Prokopyevsk.

the inflow of capital in the goods-producing industries

abro d.

Economic Issues

Yeltsin Calls For Corrections to Draft 1996 Budget

LD1310190695 Moscow INTERFAX in English
1639 GMT 13 Oct 95

(FBIS Transcribed Text) MOSCOW, Oct 13 (INTER-
FAX) — Russian President Boris Yeltsin believes fore-
casts of the country’s social and economic development
in the government-proposed budget for 1996 must be
clarified. He told a meeting of the leaders of Russian au-
tonomous republics on Friday that this was not grounded

well enough.

Yeltsin said clarifications were needed of inflation rates
and revenues from property. He called for correcting
the direction of tax reform and finding other non-
inflationary sources of servicing Russia’s foreign debt
and budget deficit.

The president diverted attention to the need for seeking
non-budgetary resources in the republics and regions,
saying that serious reform was needed in defining the
relationship of various level budgets in 1996.

First Deputy Prime Minister Anatoliy Chubays at the
same meeting announced government plans to more
than double financial aid to the republic budgets from
sume 15 trillion rubles in 1995 to 38 trillion next year.
He said the government had met its obligations on this
year’s transfers and even exceeded the target figures at
some places.

However, Chubays said that the existing system of form-
ing transfers and financial aid was causing objections
both from recipients and the government which is well
aware of the need to reform it because of being obso-
lete.

Participants in the meeting agreed with arguments
presented by the cabinet which wants to rectify some of
the imbalances. Chubays disagrees that Moscow region
is rated as one of the needy under the existing system.
"This may not be so," he said.

From now on, he said, the transfers would be made
not from a sum of 27% of the value added tax from
the federal treasury but 15% of federal tax revenues.
The government hopes the measure will help speedy
tax collection in the regions.
